It is now written off at once only where frames normally don't wait (all but one of the 32 passes before the wait began found none: the GPU-bound and the capped runs, where a pending frame after a swap is a merge), and after half a second of waiting at every pass otherwise (the longest wait of a frame ahead in those 60 s was 42 passes, 0.3 s): 7 frames written off in the same 60 s at native (0.07%), 5 at 3x (4 before). Replays with a swap dropped now and then, to make merges, heal every one of them: at once at 3x, within half a second at native. A swap with no frame, then two passes, and one swap (S T T S) would otherwise have left one frame pending for good; the usual order, T T S S, pairs as it should. `low_latency_debug` 16 counts it every 300 frames: in a 60 FPS cap run with Reflex on, 60 s, D3D12: 4 timer passes with no swap since the previous one, 2 pending frames written off after a swap (1 of them after waiting half a second), 0 after a stall, 1 swap in play with no frame, 2 ids started twice, in 3,600 frames (3, 2, 0, 1, 2 in 1,800 frames before); Vulkan: 2, 2 (1 of them after waiting half a second), 0, 1, 2 in 3,600 frames (2, 2, 0, 1, 2 in 1,790 before); native resolution, uncapped, Vulkan: 2,313 passes with no swap since the previous one and 5 written off (none after waiting); D3D12: 1,744 timer passes with no swap since the previous one, 7 pending frames written off after a swap (0 of them after waiting half a second), 0 after a stall, 7 swaps in play with no frame, 7 ids started twice (981 written off before: the rule above). The thread that makes the passes is the first the game's hook passes in; the SDK warns if a pass comes from another, or if that thread also writes swap packets. The markers: simulation start and input sample (the update thread, after the wait), simulation end (the swap packet written), render submit start (the frame's first draw, `ExecutePacketType3Draw`) and end (the swap packet executed), present start and end (around the presents of the guest's frames; none while no markers go out, so with the reducer off the submissions and presents are as they were).
- **Reflex, Direct3D 12.** No NVIDIA file is shipped or linked: `nvapi64.dll` from the driver (loaded from the system folder only) is called through `nvapi_QueryInterface` with the interface ids of NVAPI's public header (`nvapi_interface.h`, MIT). Only the few structures used (`NV_SET_SLEEP_MODE_PARAMS`, `NV_LATENCY_MARKER_PARAMS`, `NV_GET_SLEEP_STATUS_PARAMS`, the latency report, the marker enum) are copied, with `static_assert`s on their sizes. `SetSleepMode` takes the mode (on, boost, the minimum frame interval), `Sleep` is the wait at the start of the frame, `SetLatencyMarker` the markers. Nothing but `NvAPI_Initialize` is called until the mode is first set (whether the driver takes Reflex's calls for this GPU is found out then: a refusal is asked again a second later, twice, and then makes the reducer unavailable, with `NOT SUPPORTED` in the menu, and leaves the frame rate to the game's own limiter). `NvAPI_D3D_Sleep` can't be cancelled; the core times it when it returns (below), and a driver that blocks it for long enough makes the reducer give up for a while; a call that never returns is not covered (there is no bound on this one, as Vulkan's wait has).
- **Reflex, Vulkan.** `VK_NV_low_latency2` (with `VK_KHR_present_id`, its `presentId` feature and timeline semaphores): the device asks for the extensions only on an NVIDIA GPU, where `low_latency_debug_backend` allows it and `low_latency_device_extensions` is on (it is, but on the PS5: below), and drops them again if the `presentId` feature or Vulkan 1.2 is missing. The swapchain is made with `VkSwapchainLatencyCreateInfoNV`; a driver that refuses that for the first swapchain is asked again without it (the reducer is then unavailable, `SWAPCHAIN REFUSED IT`, and the game plays on). `vkSetLatencySleepModeNV` sets the mode, `vkLatencySleepNV` signals a timeline semaphore the update thread waits on (outside the swapchain's lock, bounded a little over what counts as a long wait: a quarter of a second, or four of the cap's intervals), `vkSetLatencyMarkerNV` carries the markers. Every submission of a marked frame is attributed to it with `VkLatencySubmissionPresentIdNV` (the command processor's submission thread and its direct submits keep the frame's id; measured better than leaving the driver to guess: `low_latency_debug` bit 4 turns it off; revision 3 of the extension, which the RTX 5080's driver advertises, where the vendored Vulkan header 357 describes revision 2: with an older driver the submissions are left to its own rules), and every present carries `VkPresentIdKHR`. A swapchain the backend is told was made without the latency mode (the retry covers a first swapchain; a later recreation that fails only because of it restarts the surface, whose first swapchain is retried) makes the backend give up at once, whatever the policy has told it (`SWAPCHAIN REFUSED IT`). The swapchain is told to the backend under a lock (the UI thread makes and retires it, the game's threads call the driver), and the backend is shut down (`Shutdown`, after the calls in progress) when the presenter goes. Frame generation's pacer presents on a queue of its own, declared with `vkQueueNotifyOutOfBandNV`; its real frames' presents carry the out-of-band present markers, its generated ones their own kind (so that Anti-Lag 2 counts the real frame's only), the guest frame's hand-off to it the in-band ones.
- **Anti-Lag 2, Direct3D 12** (UNTESTED: written from AMD's header and documentation, never run on a Radeon): AMD's `ffx_antilag2_dx12.h` (MIT; copied as `amd_antilag2_dx12.h` with two small changes for llvm-mingw, marked MCLA in the file: a `reinterpret_cast` where AMD's has an MSVC-only `static_cast` from `void*` to a function pointer, and the interface's `__CRT_UUID_DECL`; both valid for MSVC's Clang too) loads `amdxc64.dll` from the driver. The context is made the first time the reducer is wanted, and handed to the frame generation swap chain's private data only once `Initialize` has succeeded (the proxy's thread must not find the interface a failed `Initialize` releases; the presenter clears the pointer before the swap chain goes) (AMD's `Initialize` turns the driver's Anti-Lag off, which is not for a user who never chose it here: their Adrenalin setting stays); `Update(enable, maxFPS)` is the wait at the start of the frame, and the calls take no lock of ours (AMD's SDK is built for `Update`, `MarkEndOfFrameRendering` and `SetFrameGenFrameType` to run on three threads: a mutex held across the driver's sleep, as it once was, would stop the GPU thread's mark every frame). `MarkEndOfFrameRendering` is sent at the start of the present into the swap chain, once the frame's main work has been submitted. With frame generation the swap chain made by AMD's frame interpolation library is given the private data `IID_IFfxAntiLag2Data`, so that its proxy tells the driver which presents are generated.
- **Anti-Lag 2, Vulkan** (UNTESTED): `vkAntiLagUpdateAMD` with `VkAntiLagDataAMD` (the mode, the frame limit, and the input and present stages with the frame's index). Until the reducer is on the mode is `DRIVER_CONTROL` (the user's own Adrenalin setting decides), not `OFF`. The present stage goes just before the `vkQueuePresentKHR` of the frame's image: the swapchain's own present, or the frame generation pacer's real frame (not at the hand-off to the pacer, up to tens of ms before).
- **Absent drivers.** Every vendor path checks for what it needs and reports why not, in the menu's letters: `NO NVAPI` (no `nvapi64.dll`), `NVAPI FAILED`, `NEEDS A NEWER DRIVER`, `NO AMD DRIVER`, `NOT SUPPORTED` (the driver refused the mode), `SWAPCHAIN REFUSED IT`, `NOT ON THIS GPU`, `NEXT START` (the Vulkan device was made without the extensions: the PS5's), `TURNED OFF` (`low_latency_debug_backend=none`). With `low_latency_debug_backend` (`nvidia` or `amd`, restart) the other vendor's calls can be tried on any GPU, which is how the guards were run here (a GeForce reports `NO AMD DRIVER` on Direct3D 12 and `NEEDS A NEWER DRIVER` on Vulkan, and the game plays on).
- **A wait that takes too long.** `LowLatency::OnFrameStart` times every backend's `Sleep` (the driver's wait before the frame; Reflex's `NvAPI_D3D_Sleep` can't be cancelled). One over a quarter of a second, or over four of the frame-rate limit's intervals where that is longer (a 4 FPS cap's), is a long wait, and the game's own limiter holds the cap from it (the warning is logged at most every 5 s, with the count of the ones since). Three among the last eight waits (three in a row would never come for long, long, short, repeated) give the reducer up (`SLEEP TOO LONG` in the menu), and the count starts again whenever the reducer is not working: the driver is told to leave the mode (before, nothing was sent and it stayed in the mode, with no sleeps or markers), and the reducer is tried again after 5 s, then after 10, 20, 40 and at most 60 s while it keeps failing; for 5 s after each new start one long wait is enough to give up again (a probe, not three stalls), and after 30 s without one the pause starts at 5 s again. It was given up for the session before (a minimised or covered window, or a long hitch, could have cost the feature until the game restarted). A mode refused three times and a Vulkan swapchain made without the latency mode stay given up for the session (the log says so, and asks no more), and the driver is told to leave the mode there too; a swapchain refusal found while a pause runs replaces the reason and makes it permanent. The refusal count and the long-wait count start again with each give-up and each new try. The first two passes after the driver takes the mode make no wait: it has seen no markers of the frames before (none go out while the reducer is off), and on Vulkan its first wait after a new try took about 100 ms, once or twice (4 of 7 new tries showed it; none of 9 with the two passes). The first start and the row turned on take the same two passes, which were not measured separately. The Vulkan wait is bounded just over the long-wait limit, so a driver that never signals counts as one; Reflex's `NvAPI_D3D_Sleep` has no bound (it can't be cancelled, and a watchdog calling `SetMode(off)` from another thread, which might not release it, was not built), so a Direct3D 12 sleep that never returns hangs the update thread as it would without this patch (none was seen). `low_latency_debug` 128 adds 300 ms to every wait, to test it, and 256 with it spares every third (long, long, short). Measured here (RTX 5080, 30 FPS cap, the driver limiter, Reflex on, 12 or 15 s each): minimising the game's window, hiding it, covering it with a pop-up the game made over it (the dev pad's `window` command) and bringing it back took no long wait on either API: the swaps stayed at 30.0 a second with a 33.3 ms median in every segment, and the row stayed ON. With `low_latency_debug=128` on Direct3D 12: three waits of 330, 300 and 300 ms, `SLEEP TOO LONG` and the driver told off, asked again after 5 s (one wait of 300 ms: given up again), then after 10 s and 20 s; with the option cleared the next try took, and it stayed on; 30 s later a new round asked again after 5 s. Between the stalls the cap held (33.3 ms median in every segment, 30.0 a second).
- **Policy and settings.** `low_latency` (`auto`, `off`, `on`, `boost`; hot): `boost` is Reflex's On + Boost (the GPU clocks stay high), NVIDIA only; `auto` is on for NVIDIA Reflex and off for AMD Anti-Lag 2 (untested). The driver is told nothing until the policy has turned the reducer on, so a user who leaves it off, or has no tested reducer, keeps their driver's own setting. `low_latency_frame_generation` (`auto` or `off`, config file only): with `auto`, the reducer is on whenever frame generation is on, whatever `low_latency` says; `off` is for troubleshooting. `low_latency_limiter` (`auto`, `fork`, `driver`): who holds the game's frame-rate cap while the reducer is on. With `driver` the game's own limiter (which waits at the end of the frame, `WaitForFrameDeadline`) waits for nothing and the cap goes to the driver as the minimum frame interval, so the wait comes before the frame's input is read; `auto` is the driver where its reducer was tested (NVIDIA) and frame generation is off, and the game's own otherwise (measured below). The driver's limiter is in charge only while it is confirmed: the mode was taken and this frame's wait was made (`SetMode` and `Sleep` return whether they were); in any frame where either failed (no swapchain yet, a refused call) the game's own limiter holds the frame rate, so a failure never leaves it uncapped. A refused `SetMode` is asked again a second later, twice more, before the reducer counts as unavailable; while it waits the game's own limiter holds the cap. `low_latency_device_extensions` (Vulkan, start only; on, but off on the PS5, whose host turns it on when the game starts with `low_latency` on or `boost`, or with frame generation chosen: the console's device stays as it was otherwise). The status is the presenter's kind of value, not a cvar (`LowLatency::GetStatus`: the vendor's name, available or why not, active, forced by frame generation, whether boost exists, untested), as frame generation's is. Development: `low_latency_debug` (bit 1 sends the markers even with the reducer off, so a latency tool such as PresentMon reads them; bit 2 logs the driver's own report of the last frames every 300 frames and sends the markers; bit 4 leaves each Vulkan submission's frame to the driver; bit 8 adds the newest frames' times; bit 16 logs how the frames' ids were paired; bit 32 makes the driver refuse the mode twice and bit 64 always, to test the retry and the fall back to the game's limiter; bit 128 makes every wait before a frame 300 ms longer, to test giving the reducer up and trying again, and 256 with it only two of every three) and `low_latency_debug_backend` (`auto`, `nvidia`, `amd`, `none`; restart).
- **The names** are used as plain text to say which feature it is (`NVIDIA Reflex`, `AMD Anti-Lag 2`), without logos, and the trademark line is in `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md` with the NVAPI and Anti-Lag 2 header notices.
- **Linux and the PS5.** The Vulkan paths compile everywhere (the vendored Vulkan headers have both extensions) and are enabled where the device offers them, labelled UNTESTED in the menu, the launcher and here. Neither has been built or run; the PS5 build is covered by the same code (no NVAPI, no Direct3D 12 there). On the PS5 the device is made as before unless the game starts with the reducer asked for (`ps5/game/main_ps5.cpp` sets `low_latency_device_extensions`).
- **The FidelityFX runtime check** (also in this patch, the SDK's own fix): `TaaCheckAvailable` and the frame generation checks only tested that `amd_fidelityfx_dx12.dll` / `_vk.dll` existed, so a DLL that is there and doesn't load (a missing import: the Visual C++ runtime, or the Vulkan loader) reported FSR as available and failed at the first frame. `rex::platform::CheckLoadable` (`dynlib.h`; `LoadLibraryExW` on Windows by the file's full path with `LOAD_LIBRARY_SEARCH_DLL_LOAD_DIR | LOAD_LIBRARY_SEARCH_SYSTEM32`, so its imports come from its own folder and the system's and not from the application's or current directory; `dlopen` elsewhere) loads the file and keeps it loaded; the three checks (FSR on Direct3D 12 and on Vulkan, frame generation on both) now report it as unavailable, with the error in the log and `RUNTIME NOT LOADED` in the menu's status. The cost is the load itself (a few ms at start, where it was an existence test), and a DLL whose `DllMain` crashes would now stop the game at start and not only FSR. Checked with a copy of the game folder whose Direct3D 12 DLL was a text file (error 193) and whose Vulkan DLL had its `vulkan-1.dll` import renamed (error 126): FSR and frame generation were reported unavailable on both APIs (`FSR is unavailable here; MSAA is used instead`), the reducer stayed on for what it does on its own, and the game ran.
- **The FidelityFX DLLs' C runtime** (a line in `rexglue-temporal-aa.patch`'s `cmake/rexglue_fsr.cmake`, and a check in this patch): the two runtimes are built with the static C runtime (`CMAKE_MSVC_RUNTIME_LIBRARY=MultiThreaded`, as the release workflow builds them), so a PC needs no Visual C++ Redistributable for them. `llvm-readobj --coff-imports`: `amd_fidelityfx_dx12.dll` imports KERNEL32, USER32, WINMM and dxgi only (it had MSVCP140, VCRUNTIME140 and six `api-ms-win-crt-*` DLLs), `amd_fidelityfx_vk.dll` KERNEL32 and vulkan-1. FSR and frame generation ran on both APIs with them. A step after the build (`cmake/rexglue_fsr_check_crt.cmake`) warns when a built runtime imports the Visual C++ or universal C runtime again (a later FidelityFX pin could bring it back); checked against a dynamic-CRT DLL (it warns) and the static ones (it doesn't).
- **llvm-mingw.** The patch builds with both toolchains (the AMD header's two changes above; the SDK's `-fms-extensions` is on for mingw by `rexglue-mingw.patch`); `rexglue-mingw.patch` applies on top of it unchanged. Syntax-checked with the pinned llvm-mingw against the patched tree: the 14 files the patch changes, with the DXC, PIX and `__uuidof` fixes of `rexglue-mingw.patch` in the tree, compile with 0 errors (the full SDK build is CI's).

**Measured** (Windows 11, RTX 5080, 240 Hz G-SYNC display, 1920x1080 window, independent flip checked on every run by PresentMon (`pmgate.pad`: the 40-second steady segment of a scripted city drive), HDR on, FSR anti-aliasing; the build of this patch, runs one after the other under the GPU lock). Times are medians from the guest's swap (`swap`) or from the engine timer pass that read the frame's input (`start`, the input-to-screen time) to the frame reaching the display, per PresentMon's `DisplayLatency`. The last column is the sigma of the time between two frames' display times minus the simulation step the engine took for the second (`launcher/dev/presents.py`: zero is perfectly even motion; the noise floor of PresentMon's timestamps is about 0.3 to 0.5 ms), the number that stands for judder. Reflex off, on (the default), boost:

| | rendered / s | swap to screen (ms) | start to screen (ms) | step against display interval, sigma (ms) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1x, D3D12: off, on, boost | 145.75, 146.12, 145.97 | 2.00, 2.10, 2.12 | 9.14, 9.20, 9.24 | 0.485, 0.483, 0.472 |
| 1x, Vulkan: off, on, boost | 146.07, 146.44, 146.17 | 2.17, 2.20, 2.34 | 9.88, 9.90, 10.11 | 0.580, 0.547, 0.497 |
| 3x, D3D12: off, on, boost | 116.44, 113.29 (-2.7%), 109.74 (-5.8%) | 21.99, 8.41, 7.40 | 38.43, 15.93, 14.71 | 0.351, 0.165, 0.283 |
| 3x, Vulkan: off, on, boost | 110.14, 109.22 (-0.8%), 108.99 (-1.0%) | 24.04, 9.10, 9.15 | 41.67, 17.07, 17.14 | 0.553, 0.251, 0.232 |

At native resolution the GPU has room (54% busy), nothing queues, and the reducer changes nothing; at 3x it takes the queue away, 13.6 ms (D3D12) and 14.9 ms (Vulkan) from swap to screen and 22.5 and 24.6 ms from input to screen, and the motion gets more even, not less. **What it costs is frame rate on Direct3D 12 at 3x, 3 to 5%** with On (2.7% in this table; 4.0%, 117.51 to 112.85, in the first A/B; 4.8%, 117.53 to 111.91, in the limiter runs below) and about 6% with Boost: the game waits at the start of frames instead of keeping the GPU saturated. On Vulkan it costs about 1%, and at native resolution nothing.

With frame generation on (FSR 3.1; 3x uncapped, and the Half display cap, 116 rendered / 232 shown, which is the default; "off" is `low_latency_frame_generation=off low_latency=off`, "on" the forced reducer):

| | rendered / s | swap to screen (ms) | start to screen (ms) | sigma (ms) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3x, D3D12: off, on | 92.87, 92.50 | 23.50, 23.60 | 36.78, 36.99 | 0.192, 0.189 |
| 3x, Vulkan: off, on | 90.98, 88.48 (-2.7%) | 31.03, 18.55 | 45.68, 26.71 | 0.495, 1.051 |
| Half display, D3D12: off, on | 116.01, 116.01 | 6.27, 6.30 | 15.25, 15.23 | 0.648, 0.687 |
| Half display, Vulkan: off, on | 116.01, 116.01 | 6.40, 6.39 | 15.48, 15.48 | 0.746, 0.584 |

- **Vulkan: it works** with frame generation at 3x: the pacer's queue is declared out of band, the driver sees the guest frames only, and the swap to screen falls 12.5 ms and the input to screen 19.0 ms for 2.7% of the rendered frames. **The pacing gets worse there**: the sigma doubles (0.50 to 1.05 ms), the presents' spacing (p1 to p99) goes from 5.1 to 6.2 ms to 4.7 to 7.8 ms, and the swap to screen has a long tail (p95 32 ms against an 18.6 ms median): the driver's sleep follows the GPU's frame time, which with frame generation's pacer in front of the presents is not even. It is on there because frame generation forces it; `low_latency_frame_generation = "off"` in `mcla.toml` takes it off for someone who prefers even motion to the latency. At the default Half display cap the GPU has room and the reducer changes nothing.
- **Direct3D 12: it does nothing** with frame generation (23.50 against 23.60 ms). AMD's frame interpolation swap chain presents from a queue and a thread of its own and its `Present` blocks the caller until its pacing allows; the driver's sleep returned at once (0.00 ms waited, "game sleep used" in its status) because it never saw a frame waiting for the GPU through that queue. Tried, none made a difference: the present markers moved to just before the call into the swap chain, the present markers left out, the out-of-band present markers sent from the callback the swap chain makes for each present, `bUseMinQueueTime`; the swap chain's queue can't be reached from outside (`GetDevice` on the proxy returns `E_NOINTERFACE`), so it can't be declared out of band as the Vulkan pacer's is. The reducer is still forced on there with frame generation (it costs nothing), and the row says so (`ON (FRAME GEN)`); the numbers above are why a tuned frame-rate cap (Half display, the default, 6.3 ms) is what keeps frame generation's latency short on Direct3D 12.
- **The Vulkan latency gap** (3x with frame generation, reducer off: 31.0 ms from swap to screen on Vulkan, 23.5 ms on Direct3D 12): it is a queue, not the pacer's timing. With the Half display cap (the GPU has room) the two APIs are the same, 6.40 and 6.27 ms; the reducer changes nothing there. At 3x the Vulkan game runs further ahead of the GPU than the Direct3D 12 one, and the extra is about two thirds of a rendered frame (7.5 ms of the 11 ms interval). With Reflex on (the driver held the game back 8.9 ms per frame on average in its own report, in an earlier run) Vulkan comes out 5.0 ms ahead of Direct3D 12 (18.55 against 23.60 ms).
- **The cap, and who holds it.** Reflex on, frame generation off, 1x, the game's cap at 45, 60 and 120 FPS: the game's own limiter (`fork`, which waits at the end of the frame, after the input was read), the driver's (`driver`: the cap given to Reflex as its minimum frame interval, so that the wait comes before the frame starts), and the driver's with the wait after the engine timer had read the time (how the first version of this patch did it; `driver, late`). First, the time from reading the input to the screen, and the sigma of the step against the display interval (reducer off for reference; `-` where the driver's cap makes no difference):

| | reducer off | fork | driver | driver, late |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D3D12, 45 FPS: start to screen, sigma | 23.82 ms, 0.719 | 23.65 ms, 0.495 | 10.15 ms, 0.523 | 10.33 ms, 0.594 |
| D3D12, 60 FPS | 18.13 ms, 0.658 | 18.11 ms, 0.659 | 9.96 ms, 0.415 | 10.09 ms, 0.452 |
| D3D12, 120 FPS | 9.75 ms, 0.596 | 9.73 ms, 0.618 | 9.64 ms, 0.311 | 9.65 ms, 0.367 |
| Vulkan, 45 FPS | 23.98 ms, 0.477 | 23.84 ms, 0.483 | 10.13 ms, 0.530 | 10.09 ms, 0.619 |
| Vulkan, 60 FPS | 18.47 ms, 0.681 | 18.40 ms, 0.715 | 10.10 ms, 0.521 | 10.03 ms, 0.535 |
| Vulkan, 120 FPS | 10.20 ms, 0.622 | 10.15 ms, 0.612 | 9.89 ms, 0.349 | 9.85 ms, 0.473 |

  All of them hold the rate (45.00, 60.00, 120.0 per second) and the presents' spacing (p1 to p99 21.2 to 23.2, 15.7 to 17.6, 7.3 to 9.4 ms). The engine's own step, the dt it simulates with, has a sigma of 0.15 to 0.22 ms with the game's limiter and 0.002 to 0.035 ms with the driver's (the driver's starts are even; the game's limiter lets the timer's read jitter). The driver's limiter shows the input 8 to 14 ms newer at 45 and 60 FPS, is within the noise of the game's at 45 (0.52 against 0.50 and 0.53 against 0.48) and more even at 60 and 120, and its wait **must come before the timer reads the time**: placed after it (`driver, late`) the step was measured before the wait and was worse in all 10 pairs measured (these six and the four uncapped below), which is the shake `KNOWN-ISSUES.md` describes for a limiter at the timer. Uncapped, where the reducer's wait is all there is (no cap to hold), the same before/after: D3D12 1x 0.475 and 0.502 ms, 3x 0.168 and 0.271 (reducer off 0.269); Vulkan 1x 0.536 and 0.575, 3x 0.235 and 0.320 (reducer off 0.459). Swap to screen is 0.6 to 1.7 ms longer with the driver's limiter, because the frame goes out as soon as it is done instead of being held. With frame generation on at the Half display cap the driver's limiter was worse for the latency, not better (Vulkan 8.45 against 6.44 ms swap to screen, D3D12 9.09 against 6.29), so `auto` leaves the cap with the game there.
- **The new hook is where the wait comes from**: the engine timer's first instruction (`config/frame_timing.toml`, `mcla_frame_start`), not the instruction after it reads the time, which is where the first version of this patch put it.

**The default.** NVIDIA Reflex is **on by default** (`low_latency = auto`): where it costs frame rate (3 to 5% at 3x on Direct3D 12) it takes 13.6 ms off the swap to the screen and 22.5 ms off the input to the screen, and it costs nothing at native resolution; the cost is stated next to the gain in the README, the launcher's tip and `KNOWN-ISSUES.md`. `low_latency = off` turns it off. One constant per backend (`kOnByDefault`, in `d3d12_low_latency.cpp` and in `vulkan_low_latency.h`) is what `auto` means for NVIDIA. AMD Anti-Lag 2 is off by default too, as long as it is untested, and frame generation turns either on.

**The driver's own timing report** (`low_latency_debug` 2, `vkGetLatencyTimingsNV`, `NvAPI_D3D_GetLatency`) matches PresentMon on Direct3D 12; on Vulkan its timeline is shifted by about 5 ms against PresentMon's (the driver's clock domains and attribution), so the tables above are PresentMon's. Explicit submission attribution lowers the Vulkan numbers against the driver's implicit attribution; `VkPresentIdKHR` made no measurable difference.

**Validation.** The Vulkan validation layer with synchronization validation, frame generation and Reflex on, reported only what it reports without them, plus `UNASSIGNED-Threading-MultipleThreads-Read` on `vkSetLatencyMarkerNV`, `vkLatencySleepNV` and `vkSetLatencySleepModeNV` called while the UI thread presents or acquires on the same swapchain: the extension's own specification lets those calls run concurrently with the swapchain's other commands, and the layer does not know the carve-out (run before this revision's changes to the locking; the calls and their order are the same). Switching frame generation and HDR during play with Reflex on (which recreates the swapchain on Vulkan) did not break the sleep or the markers, on either API; with `low_latency_device_extensions=false` or `low_latency_debug_backend=none` the Vulkan device is made without the extensions (`NEXT START` and `TURNED OFF` in the status); with `low_latency=off` the driver is told nothing (no mode, no log line).

**Untested here:** AMD Anti-Lag 2 on both APIs (no Radeon: only the absent-driver paths ran, above; the marker placement follows AMD's header and the Khronos text); Linux and the PS5 (not built); a driver older than the first with `VK_NV_low_latency2` (the extension is checked, the row says `NEEDS A NEWER DRIVER`); a Vulkan driver that refuses the latency mode on the swapchain (the retry without it is written and not run); NVIDIA's other GPU generations (the 5080's driver only); the full llvm-mingw SDK build (CI builds it; here every file of the patch passed a syntax check with the pinned llvm-mingw against the tree with both mingw patches).

**Upstream:** fork-only for now (upstream work is parked); the `LowLatency` interface, the NVAPI and Vulkan backends and the FidelityFX runtime check would be separate pieces.

| Change | Files | Affects |
|---|---|---|
| The frame bookkeeping (ids from the swap packets), the policy, the cvars, the status | `include/rex/ui/low_latency.h`, `src/ui/low_latency.cpp`, `src/ui/CMakeLists.txt` | all platforms |
| The swap packet counted where it is written | `src/kernel/xboxkrnl/xboxkrnl_video.cpp` | all platforms |
| The markers in the command processor (render submit, the swap) | `src/graphics/command_processor.cpp` | all platforms |
| Reflex and Anti-Lag 2 on Direct3D 12 (NVAPI loader and structures, AMD's header, the swap chain's private data), the markers around the presents | `include/rex/ui/d3d12/d3d12_low_latency.h`, `src/ui/d3d12/d3d12_low_latency.cpp`, `include/rex/ui/d3d12/amd_antilag2_dx12.h`, `include/rex/ui/d3d12/d3d12_presenter.h`, `src/ui/d3d12/d3d12_presenter.cpp`, `src/ui/d3d12/d3d12_presenter_frame_generation.cpp` | D3D12 |
| Reflex and Anti-Lag 2 on Vulkan: the device extensions and features (only for the vendor, not on the PS5 until asked), the swapchain (retried without the latency mode), the sleep, the markers, the submissions' attribution, the pacer's out-of-band queue | `include/rex/ui/vulkan/vulkan_low_latency.h`, `src/ui/vulkan/vulkan_low_latency.cpp`, `include/rex/ui/vulkan/functions/device_ext_nv_low_latency2.inc`, `device_ext_amd_anti_lag.inc`, `include/rex/ui/vulkan/device.h`, `src/ui/vulkan/vulkan_device.cpp`, `include/rex/ui/vulkan/presenter.h`, `src/ui/vulkan/vulkan_presenter.cpp`, `src/ui/vulkan/vulkan_presenter_frame_generation.cpp`, `include/rex/graphics/vulkan/command_processor.h`, `src/graphics/vulkan/command_processor.cpp` | Vulkan (all platforms) |
| The FidelityFX runtimes are loaded to be checked; the check that they link the C runtime in | `include/rex/platform/dynlib.h`, `src/core/dynlib_win.cpp`, `src/core/dynlib_posix.cpp`, `src/graphics/d3d12/temporal_aa.cpp`, `src/graphics/vulkan/temporal_aa.cpp`, `src/ui/vulkan/vulkan_device.cpp`, `src/ui/d3d12/d3d12_presenter_frame_generation.cpp`, `cmake/rexglue_fsr.cmake`, `cmake/rexglue_fsr_check_crt.cmake` (new) | all platforms; Windows |

## rexglue-frame-generation-nonwindows.patch

**Why.** Frame generation (`rexglue-frame-generation.patch`) is offered on Linux and the PS5 as FSR 3.1 (UNTESTED), with the same Vulkan presenter and command processor code as on Windows, but until now FidelityFX's runtime was built there without frame generation's components (optical flow, frame interpolation, the provider), the Vulkan device checked nothing about the runtime off Windows (the command processor found out when it made the context), and the PS5's fixed-refresh display was paced like a variable-refresh one. This patch is phase 5 of the frame generation plan. **Nothing in it has been built or run on Linux or on a console.**

**What it changes.**
- **The runtime has frame generation on every platform.** `cmake/fidelityfx/CMakeLists.txt` sets `FFX_API_ENABLE_FRAMEGEN_PROVIDER` for the Vulkan backend whatever the platform (it was Windows only), with the same stand-in for AMD's Vulkan frame-interpolation swapchain (Win32-only, manual mode needs none), unless `REXGLUE_FSR_FRAMEGEN=OFF` (an option of `cmake/rexglue_fsr.cmake`, passed down; `ps5/make_ps5.sh` takes it as `MCLA_PS5_FRAMEGEN=0`): upscaling only, as before. On Windows nothing changes. The components' sources have nothing Windows-specific in them (searched the pinned FidelityFX checkout: `windows.h`, `_WIN32`, `__declspec`, `OutputDebugString`, Direct3D and DXGI names: none in frame interpolation, optical flow or the frame generation provider), the Vulkan backend is the one FSR's upscaler already builds on Linux, and the PS5 gets them in the static libraries it links (`libffx_frameinterpolation_x64.a`, `libffx_opticalflow_x64.a` with `libamd_fidelityfx_vk.a`; the title's link already takes every `libffx_*.a`, and make_ps5's audit of the Vulkan functions they call directly covers them; it now says so when the two are missing).
- **The device's runtime check** (`vulkan_device.cpp`, where Windows already loaded `amd_fidelityfx_vk.dll`): on Linux `libamd_fidelityfx_vk.so` next to the executable is loaded and kept (`CheckLoadable`: `NO FIDELITYFX RUNTIME` if missing, `RUNTIME NOT LOADED` if it doesn't load, as on Windows); on the PS5 (`REX_FSR_STATIC`) that FidelityFX is linked into the title (weak references to `ffxCreateContext` and `ffxDispatch`, as the GPU plugin's, which are null when `ps5/game/build.sh` linked the game without the archives); a build without FSR says `NO FIDELITYFX RUNTIME`. `rexui` gets `REX_HAS_FSR` and `REX_FSR_STATIC` as the GPU plugin does. The row of the pause menu on both is FSR 3.1 (UNTESTED), and FSR 3.1 (NEXT START) where the PS5's device was made without FSR's features.
- **The PS5's pacing** (`frame_generation_pacing`: `auto`, `timed`, `display`; untested on the console). AMD's presenter spaces a generated and a real frame by half the rendered interval, with its own waits, which suits a variable-refresh display. The PS5's `VK_KHR_display` has a fixed refresh (59.94 Hz) and a tear-free (FIFO) swapchain, which blocks the pacer's acquire until the display is ready: with `display` the pacer presents the two frames back to back and the swapchain spaces them at the refresh, while the back-pressure the pacer already has (the game's thread waits until the pacer has taken the frame) holds the game to half of the refresh rate, and the game's limiter (30 FPS, just above 29.97) never has to. `auto` is `display` on the PS5 and `timed` elsewhere; `timed` brings the PC pacing back on the console, `display` tries the console's on a PC (it needs the tear-free swapchain, `frame_generation_present = auto`).
- **Overrides and where it is off.** Off by default (`frame_generation = off`). A Linux or PS5 build leaves frame generation out of FidelityFX with `REXGLUE_FSR_FRAMEGEN=OFF` (`MCLA_PS5_FRAMEGEN=0`) and FSR altogether with `REXGLUE_ENABLE_FSR=OFF` (`MCLA_PS5_FSR=0`). If FidelityFX fails to build with frame generation, `ps5/make_ps5.sh` builds the runtime again without it (and remembers that in the build folder, `.framegen-failed`, until `MCLA_PS5_FRAMEGEN=1` is given) so that FSR anti-aliasing is kept; the CMake warning for a runtime that failed to build, and the title link's fallback message, name the option (a Linux build is configured again by hand). The retry's shell logic was run with stand-ins for `cmake` and `ninja` (a first build that fails with frame generation and succeeds without, a second run, an explicit `MCLA_PS5_FRAMEGEN=1`, a build that fails both ways, FSR off); no console toolchain was run. The PS5's device gets FSR's features, and so frame generation's, only when the game starts with FSR anti-aliasing or frame generation chosen (NEXT START, as in the earlier patches). `frame_generation_debug_fail` and the rest of the development switches work as on Windows.

**Checked, and not.**
- **Compiled and run on Windows:** the SDK builds with it, FSR and frame generation run on both APIs (the Windows runtimes are the same build as before: frame generation was already on there). `launcher/dev/fsr/static_test`, which links FidelityFX as static libraries from this wrapper the way the PS5's title does (the plugin's `temporal_aa_static.cpp` as the only source of the Vulkan functions, no Vulkan library, clang with `-flto=thin`), builds and passes on the GPU: FSR 3.1 upscaling for 8 frames and frame generation for 4, the output of a still picture equal to the input. `frame_generation_pacing=display` on the 240 Hz Windows display, Vulkan with frame generation at the Half display cap: every refresh presented once, the pacer's presents equal to the swaps; PresentMon was not capturing at that time, so the spacing of the presents was not measured.
- **Syntax-checked with the platform forced** (a copy of `rex/platform.h` with Windows' predefines off and Linux's or the PS5's on; llvm-mingw's clang and libc++ with stand-in headers for what mingw lacks of POSIX: `dlfcn.h`, `ucontext.h`, Wayland and XCB types, `posix_memalign`, `locale_t`): for Linux `ui/low_latency.cpp`, `core/dynlib_posix.cpp`, the Vulkan `vulkan_low_latency.cpp`, `vulkan_device.cpp` (the `.so` branch, with `REX_HAS_FSR`), `vulkan_presenter.cpp` and `vulkan_presenter_frame_generation.cpp` compile with 0 errors; for the PS5 (with `REX_FSR_STATIC`) the same files plus `xboxkrnl_video.cpp`, `graphics/vulkan/frame_generation.cpp`, `graphics/vulkan/temporal_aa.cpp` and `graphics/temporal_aa.cpp` compile with 0 errors. Not compiled: Linux's `xboxkrnl_video.cpp`, `frame_generation.cpp`, `temporal_aa.cpp` (the harness' libc++ lacks `clock_cast`, which Linux's libstdc++ has; the PS5 variants of the same files do compile), and `graphics/vulkan/command_processor.cpp` on both (a glslang macro of mingw's secure API that `rexglue-mingw.patch` undoes, absent from this tree). The same files with both mingw patches on top compile with 0 errors with the pinned llvm-mingw.
- **Not built, not linked, not run:** FidelityFX's frame generation components with Linux's or the PS5's toolchain and libraries, the PS5's title link with the two new archives, the Linux `.so`, and everything at run time on both. A review for platform assumptions found: the pacer's high-resolution timer, thread priority and the dump's `fopen_s` are guarded by `REX_PLATFORM_WIN32` with portable fallbacks (a plain sleep and spin); `queue_family.may_support_presentation` is true off Windows without asking the driver (the pacer's queue choice falls back to the graphics family's second queue or the shared queue); the display's refresh rate is read on Windows only, so HALF DISPLAY is unavailable on Linux and the PS5 and the player's own cap applies (the PS5's 59.94 Hz would not offer it anyway: under 100 Hz).

**Upstream:** fork-only for now; the device check and the pacing mode are small and portable.

| Change | Files | Affects |
|---|---|---|
| Frame generation's components in the runtime on every platform; the option | `cmake/fidelityfx/CMakeLists.txt`, `cmake/rexglue_fsr.cmake` | Linux, the PS5 (Windows unchanged) |
| The device's runtime check off Windows; `rexui` knows what the runtime is | `src/ui/vulkan/vulkan_device.cpp`, `src/ui/CMakeLists.txt` | Linux, the PS5 |
| The pacer on a fixed-refresh display | `src/ui/vulkan/vulkan_presenter_frame_generation.cpp` | the PS5 (and a PC with `frame_generation_pacing=display`) |

## rexglue-threaded-submission-d3d12-idle-wait.patch

**Why.** A fix for `rexglue-threaded-submission-d3d12.patch`. On the Radeon of a Ryzen 7 9800X3D PC (D3D12, adapter 1, the integrated GPU), the game showed, for 2 to 3 frames at a time and at the same places of a deterministic route, a whitish translucent veil over the scene in blocks of about 8x8 pixels (at 720p; on dark walls and roads it looked like flat beige, tile-shaped areas). `--d3d12_threaded_submission=false` removed it, and the SDK without the threaded submission never showed it. The D3D12 debug layer reported no errors in either mode.

**What it changes.** The GPU command thread now waits until the submission thread has put its lists in the queue whenever it is about to spin or wait for more guest commands (`CommandProcessor::PrepareForWait`: its idle loop, which spins with `SwitchToThread` and then waits, and the long waits on a register or memory value). It has nothing else to process then, so none of the overlap with the replay is lost; the wait is short (about 0.4 ms on average, about 0.6 times per swap). Cvar `d3d12_threaded_submission_idle_wait` (default on; off is the behaviour before this patch; can change at run time).

**What is known, and what is not.** The exact cause is not known. The veil needs the GPU command thread to spin in its idle loop while the submission thread replays and submits the list of the guest command buffer that has just ended (an extra thread burning a core did not bring it back). Measured on the Radeon, on the 12 seconds of the route where the veil came back in every threaded run of a longer one (`blink-drive5.pad` of the scratch tests, the first stretch of the longer route), counting events of at least 100 changed 32-pixel blocks in a 60 FPS capture, per run:
- shipped behaviour (cvar off, or without the patch): 4, 3, 3, 1, 2, 4 (mean 2.8).
- with this patch: 0, 0. In a scratch build with a diagnostics switchboard, the same wait at the idle entry: 0, 0, and with a wait at every packet start too: 0, 0; a wait right after the hand-over of the last list of every guest command buffer: 0, 0, 0, 0; a 3 or 10 ms sleep right after it: 0, 0, and 3 ms right before it: 0, 0; an idle spin of 5 or 50 iterations instead of 500: 0, 0, and no spin at all: 0; a wait after every hand-over (the "d1" of the notes): 0, and 0, 0 on the longer route, like the unthreaded mode.
- no effect (1 to 7 events a run): a UAV or aliasing barrier at the start of every list; the submission thread waiting for the GPU after every list; the command thread waiting for the submission thread at the start of every draw, copy, swap, occlusion packet, submission start or fence check, or at every packet start; deferring the write-back of the read pointer to after the list is queued; a store fence at the hand-over; a higher priority or a fixed processor for the submission thread; asynchronous pipelines off; memexport readback off. An extra thread burning a core with the wait in place: 1 weak event.
- Spinning with only `SwitchToThread`, or only a memory fence, was as bad as `MaybeYield`; spinning with `_mm_pause` (never giving the core away) once ended in `DEVICE_REMOVED` within seconds, a hung GPU, which says the damage is real GPU-side garbage and not an artefact of the capture.
- The submission thread is not falling behind: its replay time (about 0.25 ms on average, 10 ms at most) and its latency from the hand-over (about 50 us) are about the same with and without the veil, and making it slower (a 2 ms sleep before the replay or before `ExecuteCommandLists`, one run) did not make the veil worse.

Then checked on the longer route and on the RTX 5080 (2026-10-27): on the Radeon, the whole deterministic route (about 75-90 s of driving), A-B-B-A, events of at least 100 blocks per run: with this patch 0 and 0; shipped behaviour 3 and 7; the v0.1.2 release build 3 (the fixed runs' largest events, checked by eye, were the game's own exposure changes, not the veil). On the RTX 5080 (D3D12, native, uncapped, the same route), A-B-B-A: drive median 6.85 and 6.82 ms with the patch against 6.83 and 6.87 shipped; the GPU command thread's CPU time per swap 3.33 and 3.03 ms against 3.12 and 3.21 shipped, and 3.71 ms with the threaded submission off, so its gain remains. The D3D12 debug layer, a short run on the Radeon in each mode: no errors and no corruption messages. Not measured: Vulkan, which this patch does not touch (it has its own threaded submission, `rexglue-threaded-submission-vulkan.patch`, and may have the same idle spin). Built and run on Windows only; the PS5's list names the patch so that the lists stay in step (D3D12 code, not built there).

| Change | Files | Affects |
|---|---|---|
| The command thread waits for the submission thread before it spins or waits for commands | `include/rex/graphics/d3d12/command_processor.h`, `src/graphics/d3d12/command_processor.cpp` | D3D12, only with threaded submission on |

## rexglue-fsr-upscaling.patch

**Why.** The SDK draws everything at `resolution_scale` times the console's 720p (3x is 4K), and at 3x the scene's pixel work is most of the frame on a weak graphics card. AMD FSR 3.1 can rebuild a full-size picture from a smaller one over several frames (it is already in the game as the anti-aliasing of `rexglue-temporal-aa.patch`, at native resolution), so this patch lets the 3D scene be drawn smaller and FSR upscale it, while the HUD, the menus and the post-processing (bloom, the tone map, the exposure) stay at the output size and sharp. It is an extension of FSR anti-aliasing: native AA is the ratio 1, and with `temporal_aa_upscale = 1` (the default) nothing of it runs. A new patch, not a revision of `rexglue-temporal-aa.patch`, which is in shipped installs.

**What it does.**

- **Which frames.** With `temporal_aa = fsr` and `temporal_aa_upscale` above 1, a frame is squeezed when FSR exists for the render size, the anti-aliasing draw was seen in the frame before, the scene's first draw is single-sampled and its surface pitch is the output's width (host render target path: not on rasterizer-ordered views, where upscaling reports unavailable). The pause menu's and the map's worlds (three tiles of 4x MSAA) are not squeezed, nor jittered, and FSR doesn't run in them; the first squeezed frame after them drops FSR's history. A frame that asks for upscaling and isn't squeezed isn't jittered either (it shows the scene unscaled, without anti-aliasing, as the first half second after a ratio change does while FSR is made for the new size).
- **The squeeze** (`TaaSqueeze`, both backends' viewport and scissor code): the host viewport and scissor of every draw between `kSceneBegin` and `kSceneEnd` whose surface pitch is the scene's are scaled about the render target's origin, `round(v * render / output)` on the edges, so the scene goes into the top-left render-size part of the output-sized render targets (their memory is unchanged). The NDC and every system constant stay those of the full viewport, which is what makes a smaller viewport draw the same picture; the jitter is in render pixels and FSR's phase count is 8 x ratio squared (18 at 1.5x, 32 at 2x).
- **The resolves** (`TaaResolveStretch`): the scene's resolves of the squeezed target, in the scene and in the post phase until the tone map, read the render target through a nearest-neighbour stretch, destination pixel p from source `((2p + 1) * render) / (2 * output)`, so every texture the game makes of the scene is a full-size picture (blocky) and everything that reads one by a full-frame UV (the ~520 draws per frame that read the day shadow mask through projected UVs, the soft particles' and fog's depth, the exposure and bloom downsamples, the motion blur's inputs) is unchanged. In this game's FSR mode the scene is one surface and four resolves are the scene's: the shadow mask (32 bpp, a "full" copy shader), the depth twice (32 bpp, the fast shader) and the HDR colour (64 bpp, a full shader, 7e3 to 16_16_16_16 with its exponent bias); the other 15 or so are before the scene (other pitches) and are left as they are. Eight new direct-resolve shader variants (`resolve_direct_{fast,full}_{32,64}bpp_stretch[_scaled]`, both APIs, made lazily the first time they are needed) add the stretch to `resolve_direct.xesli`, which the shipped shaders compile unchanged (byte-identical to the build before); the stretch is two more root constants (D3D12) and push constants (Vulkan). A scene resolve that can't be stretched (the 128 bpp shaders, a source several render targets own) makes upscaling give up for the session (`temporal_aa_upscale_available = no`, logged once), and the game goes on with FSR at native resolution.
- **The FSR step.** At the anti-aliasing draw, as before, but `taa_upscale_gather_mcla.cs` (a render-size dispatch) reads the stretched depth, stencil and scene textures at `d(i) = (2 * output * i + render - 1) / (2 * render)`, the first output pixel whose stretch maps to render pixel i (exact, the same integer function), and writes the motion vectors, the colour and the depth at render size; FSR gets `renderSize` and `upscaleSize`, the context made for the render size (`maxRenderSize`), a new one when the ratio changes (about 0.6 s of unscaled frames the first time), and its output goes into the scene texture as in native mode. The gather reads the scene through a view of its own: the texture cache's binding (its swizzle and sign variants) gave a dark colour input. The ratio is read once per frame.
- **Shaders that see the smaller viewport.** Squeezed draws sample textures with a LOD bias of -log2(ratio) (`TaaLodBias`, in the sampler's existing `lod_bias` field, a D3D12 `MipLODBias` and Vulkan `mipLodBias`, in 1/8 levels), FSR's recommendation, so the mips are those of the full-size scene. `ps_param_gen`'s pixel position (the vehicle shader and one race shader use it) is multiplied by the output over the render size: a new system constant (`ps_param_gen_scale`, 1 when nothing is squeezed) that both translators multiply it by. The constant takes the one free dword of the DXBC system constants block and is a new member of the SPIR-V one, so the translated code of every shader changes (the block's description; the multiplication is only in the shaders that use `ps_param_gen`) and the driver compiles each pipeline once more after an update, which the launcher's shader preparation does; the shader storage and the shader pack hold guest code and pipeline descriptions, not translated code, and are not invalidated.
- **Frame generation** with a squeezed scene: its depth copy takes the gathered render-size depth (`FgCopyDepth`); the presenter already takes the render size from the vectors.
- **Not done in this patch:** the reactive mask is off while upscaling (it would have to be made at the render size), and FSR's output is never replaced by a plain resample when the anti-aliasing draw isn't found in a squeezed frame (a scene type the game didn't have before: the frame is shown stretched, blocky, once; the next isn't squeezed, and three of those in a row give upscaling up); no such frame was seen in gameplay, the pause menu, the map or the race. The shadow cascades, the environment map and the other "pre" targets stay at the output scale (squeezing them is a follow-up with the same machinery), as do the transfers and clears of the render targets, the resolves, the texture loads and the post-processing.
- **Settings.** `temporal_aa_upscale` (the ratio of the output to the render size, 1 to 3, 1 by default: no upscaling; the game sets it from `mcla_upscaling`: 4/3 Ultra Quality, 1.5 Quality, 1.7 Balanced, 2 Performance; transient and hot-reloaded like `temporal_aa`; 3, Ultra Performance, only by hand; the render size is the output's over the ratio, rounded, so 4/3 is exactly three quarters of the width and height and the game passes it to the digits of a double) and `temporal_aa_upscale_available` (`unknown`, `yes`, `no`, set by the plugin at the first frame). Development: `temporal_aa_debug` 7 logs what the scene's draws and resolves do (`UPS:` lines: draws squeezed, resolves stretched, the render and output sizes), 8 gives FSR the full-size scene texture in place of the gathered colour (a check of the gather), 9 puts the gathered colour in the corner of the scene texture instead of FSR's output, 10 gives FSR the camera planes the other way round (FSR's inverted-depth check wants near > far; the shipped native AA gives it near < far and FSR logs that with its debug checking, `temporal_aa_debug 1`).

Checked on Windows, RTX 5080, at 3x (4K output; the scene is drawn at 2560x1440 at 1.5x and at 1920x1080 at 2x) unless noted, both APIs, the game's time stopped for the stills (`mcla_freeze`):

- **It runs.** `UPS: wanted true armed true state 1 ... scene draws 1,366 squeezed 1,366, jittered 1,365 (the fog quad is the one left), resolves stretched 4 other 15, render 2560x1440 output 3840x2160` (1920x1080 at 2), on D3D12 and on Vulkan, in the city by day; transitions 1 to 1.5 to 2 to 1 and back to MSAA are clean (one squeezed frame ended without the FSR pass when the ratio was read in the middle of a frame; fixed by reading it once), the pause menu's frames are not squeezed and gameplay afterwards is squeezed again, a drive with the pad (throttle, steering, the pause menu, back) likewise; no "giving up" in any run.
- **The picture.** Brightness is the same in every mode (mean of the 4K still: FSR native 33.46, Quality 33.41, Performance 33.33, MSAA 33.68; the earlier runs 39.68 / 39.56 / 39.52 / 39.91 in another time of day). Switching back to native AA gives the native picture (mean |difference| 0.12, 0.34 and 0.24 in three runs, 0.02%, 0.35% and 0.21% of pixels off by more than 8 of 255; two stills of one mode differ by 0.1 to 0.35). Against FSR at native resolution, Quality differs by a mean |difference| of 0.71 (1.2% of the pixels by more than 8), Performance 0.95 (2.0%); MSAA against native 0.87 (2.6%). Sharpness, the mean |Laplacian| of the 4K still: native FSR 4.42, Quality 3.47 (22% less), Performance 2.95 (33% less), MSAA 5.63; on Vulkan 4.32, 3.41, 2.95, 5.53. No seams: the mean difference to native in the last 16 columns and rows and across the squeezed rectangle's edge is inside the picture's average; the speedometer (not squeezed) is as sharp as at native; shadows, fog and soft particles are aligned. The player's car (the vehicle shader reads `ps_param_gen`) has the same paint, windscreen and light reflections at 1.5x and 2x as at native, in the crops.- **Validation.** The D3D12 debug layer (`--d3d12_debug=true`, captured with DBWIN: it writes to OutputDebugString) over the city look pad and a drive (squeezed frames included), 1.5x and 2x, with frame generation on at 60 FPS: no errors and no corruption; only the usual warnings (initial states ignored for buffers, MIN/MAX blend factors, live objects at exit). One warning of this patch's own (the depth input was transitioned twice in one barrier call by the gather and the frame generation copy: `ResourceBarrier: Called on the same subresource(s) of Resource 'FSR upscaling depth input' in separate Barrier Descs`, 1,022 times in the frame generation run) was fixed and is gone in the same run.
- **The planes.** FSR's debug checking says `FFX_FSR3UPSCALER_ENABLE_DEPTH_INVERTED flag is present yet cameraNear is less than cameraFar` every frame, with native AA too (`rexglue-temporal-aa.patch` gives near < far for inverted depth). With the planes the other way round (`temporal_aa_debug 10`) the picture is the same within the run-to-run noise (native: mean |difference| 0.48 against the default and 0.35 between two default stills; at 1.5x 0.30 and 0.79; |Laplacian| 4.47 against 4.40 and 4.44, 3.47 against 3.41 and 3.39): FSR takes the smaller and the larger of the two itself, so the message is only a message and the order is left as it is.
- **GPU time**, PresentMon's GPU busy per present, medians of the two or three 8-second segments of one frozen view, alternating the modes (native, Quality, Performance, native, ... then MSAA), display awake, every run valid: at 3x on D3D12 FSR native AA 9.39 ms, Quality 8.59, Performance 8.22, MSAA 8.41; on Vulkan 9.44, 8.57, 8.17, 8.29. So Quality saves 0.8 ms (8.5%) and Performance 1.2 ms (12.5%) against FSR at native resolution, and Performance is 0.2 ms under MSAA. At 2x on D3D12 the card is not GPU bound (85 to 90% busy; 142 swaps a second): 6.15, 5.80, 5.64 and 5.78 ms. The saving is what scales with the scene's pixel count; the shadow cascades (1.78 ms by day), the environment map, the transfers and clears, the resolves, the texture loads, the post-processing and the HUD stay at the output scale (`launcher/dev/frame-structure-2026-10-16.md`), and FSR's own pass costs what it did at native (1.4 ms at 3x).
- **Frame generation with it on** (D3D12, `frame_generation = fsr`, 60 FPS rendered, FSR native, 1.5x, 2x, a drive, off again): the squeezed frames are generated from (the log shows `state 1 done true`), the stills are right, the debug layer has no errors.
- **HDR on** (`hdr = on` with the display in HDR mode, so the game's scene is in HDR from the tone map's inputs; D3D12): the same look pad gives the same pictures as in SDR (mean 33.52 FSR native, 33.56 Quality, 33.50 Performance, 33.77 MSAA; |Laplacian| 4.24, 3.32, 2.79, 5.55; Quality against native 0.72 mean |difference|), no errors.
- **A race at night and in the rain** (the arcade race Sunset and Vine from its start at Midnight Clear and at Noon Rainy; D3D12 at 3x; the game's own `mcla_upscaling`, through the pad's `set`, so the menu's path is the one used): the `UPS:` lines are those of the city (1,940 to 2,370 scene draws, all squeezed, 3 resolves stretched in a race and 14 others) and nothing is given up; the mean brightness is the same in every mode (night 48.07 FSR native, 48.12 Quality, 47.86 Performance, 48.08 MSAA; rain 63.96, 63.80, 63.72, 63.99) and the sharpness falls as in the city (mean |Laplacian| at night 6.73, 4.67, 3.71 and MSAA 7.88; in rain 7.53, 5.26, 4.16 and 7.20). In the crops the player's car with its lights, the start marker's smoke plume, the wet road's reflections and the traffic lights look right at Quality and Performance, and a few seconds of driving at Quality showed no ghosting on the player's car or the opponents at the size of a contact sheet. The race's world does not stand still under `mcla_freeze` (the clock does; the smoke and the lights animate and the car creeps), so the stills of the modes differ by more than the city's do and are not compared pixel for pixel here.
- **The Radeon iGPU** (the 2-CU integrated GPU of the test machine, D3D12 and Vulkan, `--hdr=off`, windowed): PresentMon's GPU busy per present, the same alternating frozen view (`a-perf-weak.pad`, 12-second segments), display awake, every run valid, medians of the two or three segments of each mode. At 2x (1440p output) on D3D12 FSR native AA 204.4 ms (segments 203.7 to 207.2), Quality 175.1, Performance 147.1, MSAA 152.9; on Vulkan 210.6, 173.9, 154.9, 145.9. At 1x (720p output) on D3D12 45.2, 39.1, 36.5, 39.6. Quality saves 14% (Vulkan 17%) and Performance 28% (27%) at 2x, 14% and 19% at 1x. Only the main scene gets smaller: the shadow cascades and the environment map are still drawn at the 2x scale, and the post-processing, the tone map and FSR's own pass run at the output size, which is most of the 147 ms (a GPU timestamp profile of the same machine at 2x with MSAA: 46 ms before the scene, 22 ms of post-processing, 17 ms of tone mapping). So on this GPU upscaling brings FSR anti-aliasing down to about the cost of MSAA (Performance 4% under MSAA at 2x on D3D12, 6% over on Vulkan, 8% under at 1x); it doesn't make 2x playable.
- **The game side:** the pause menu's ANTI ALIASING row (pad `c-menu.pad`, then `d-menu.pad` with the Ultra Quality level, the scratch `mcla.toml`) steps through its seven values, MSAA, FSR 3.1 NATIVE AA, FSR ULTRA Q. 1080P, FSR QUALITY 960P, FSR BALANCED 847P, FSR PERFORM. 720P at 2x (1620P, 1440P, 1271P and 1080P at 3x) and wraps to OFF and back; each step sets `mcla_anti_aliasing` and `mcla_upscaling` (`temporal_aa_upscale` 1, 1.3333333333333333, 1.5, 1.7, 2; OFF and MSAA leave `mcla_upscaling` as it was, as designed) and the saved `mcla.toml` holds the last choice. A value's text is clipped at about 19 capital letters in a row, so the labels are short (`FSR 3.1 QUALITY 1440P` lost its end); the arrow after the longest is cut by the panel's edge (at 2x after FSR ULTRA Q. 1080P and FSR BALANCED 847P, the text whole; at 3x it was after BALANCED and PERFORM.). The launcher's FSR upscaling combo (with Ultra Quality) and its Screen scaling filter combo (`launcher/src/GameSettings.cs`): the launcher project builds with them (`dotnet build` of `launcher/src/MclaLauncher.csproj`, 0 errors); the combos themselves were not looked at in the running launcher (`launcher/dev/shaderpack/launcher-check` doesn't build on the unchanged fork: `Backend.cs` needs `Setup` from a file its project doesn't list).
- **Ultra Quality (4/3x), checked** (the game's own `mcla_upscaling = ultra_quality`, set through the pad's `set`; `d-ultra.pad`, free roam by day, the game's time stopped; D3D12 unless noted): the `UPS:` line says `render 1920x1080 output 2560x1440` at 2x (D3D12 and Vulkan), `render 2880x1620 output 3840x2160` at 3x and `render 960x540 output 1280x720` at 1x, with 1,359 to 1,367 scene draws all squeezed, 4 resolves stretched and nothing given up (the game hands the SDK 4/3 to the digits of a double, so the sizes are exact, not off by one). At 2x the still has the brightness of the others (mean 33.48; FSR native AA 33.55, Quality 33.42, MSAA 33.84) and a |Laplacian| of 4.51 against 5.54 for FSR native AA (19% less), 4.37 at Quality and 6.13 with MSAA (the 3x still 3.56, 3.44, 4.41 and 5.71, 19% less than native AA; the 1x still 6.90, 6.81, 8.28 and 9.36; Vulkan at 2x 4.51, 4.18, 5.47 and 6.02); the mean |difference| to FSR native AA is 0.65 (0.89% of the pixels off by more than 8 of 255; Quality 0.79 and 1.24%; native AA again 0.16 and 0.04%). The player's car (paint, windscreen, brake light) shows no screen-space artefact next to native AA and Quality in crops of the 2x still. The D3D12 debug layer (`--d3d12_debug=true`, the same pad at 2x) reports no errors and no corruption, only the creation-state warnings of the earlier runs (`CREATERESOURCE_STATE_IGNORED`, the blend-operation pair and the unnumbered ones). GPU time was not measured for this level (it draws more pixels than Quality and fewer than native AA).
- **The presenter's scaling filters** (`present_effect`; the launcher's Screen scaling filter). The SDK's presenter has CAS and FSR 1 as pixel shaders (their bytecode is in `src/ui/shaders`) but compiles them only where `REX_HAS_FIDELITYFX_SDK` is defined, which its CMake does only with `REXGLUE_ENABLE_FIDELITYFX` (that fetches the whole FidelityFX source); the fork builds with `REXGLUE_ENABLE_FSR` instead, so `present_effect` could only be `bilinear` here (the built `rexruntime.dll` listed `bilinear` as the one allowed value). The patch defines the macro for `rexui` and, so that the presenter's classes have one layout in the headers, for `rexgpu-xenos`, on Windows with `REXGLUE_ENABLE_FSR` (`src/ui/CMakeLists.txt`, `src/graphics/CMakeLists.txt`); the code under it needs nothing more. `fsr2` and `fsr3`, the experimental temporal paths, need FidelityFX's runtime linked into the presenter, which the fork's build doesn't do, so the game offers `bilinear`, `fsr` and `cas`. Linux and the PS5 are as before (bilinear only; the game's own sources, which see the headers without the macro, touch the presenter only through static and virtual functions). What each does, from `GetGuestOutputPaintFlow` (`src/ui/presenter.cpp`; the source is the game's output, scene and HUD, `resolution_scale` times 1280x720, and the target is the window):
  - `bilinear`: one bilinear stretch (a copy at the same size).
  - `cas`: Contrast Adaptive Sharpening. One pass to the smaller of twice the picture's size and the target, along each axis: `kCasSharpen` at the same size, `kCasResample` enlarging up to 2x or shrinking (the code's comment: "sharpen or downsample, or upsample up to 2x2"); a bigger enlargement finishes with a bilinear stretch. `present_cas_additional_sharpness` (0 to 1, 0 by default) adds sharpness.
  - `fsr`: where the target is larger than the picture along at least one axis, EASU passes (each at most doubles the size, up to `present_fsr_max_upsampling_passes`, 4 by default and at most), then RCAS at the target size (`present_fsr_sharpness_reduction` in stops, 0.2 by default, 0 to 2: higher is less sharp), then a bilinear stretch if the target was clamped to the largest texture; otherwise (the same size, or smaller along both axes) exactly the `cas` path. So when the picture is shrunk (2x on a 1080p screen) `fsr` and `cas` are the same single pass, a CAS resample, and `bilinear` is a plain bilinear shrink.
  - HDR output (the picture then holds HDR10 code values) and frame generation (the pacer paints the real and the generated frames in the present callback) always scale bilinearly, the filters being made for SDR values.
  - The paint configuration is built when the presenter is made, so the value is read at start (`kRequiresRestart`): the launcher and `mcla.toml` have it, the pause menu doesn't.
  - Checked on Windows, RTX 5080, both APIs, a frozen frame of free roam, the window at 1920x1080 in Windows' units, which is 2880x1620 pixels at the desktop's 150% scaling (so every run enlarged the picture: 2.25x from 1x, 1.125x from 2x; the shrinking case, 2x on a 1080p screen at 100% scaling, was not run): the window copy against the guest picture enlarged by a plain bilinear stretch, mean |difference| and the window's mean |Laplacian| (detail): D3D12 at 1x bilinear 0.48 / 1.96 (against 1.80 for the reference stretch), CAS 0.98 / 2.10, FSR 1 1.60 / 4.22; at 2x bilinear 0.33 / 3.69, CAS 1.05 / 4.85, FSR 1 1.94 / 8.56; Vulkan at 1x CAS 0.92 / 2.02, FSR 1 1.64 / 4.39. FSR 1 gives the sharpest edges and CAS a lighter sharpening, both without halos or other artefacts in the crops of the scene and the HUD; the log confirms the value in use (`present_effect`). Not measured: their GPU time.
- **Linux and the PS5** can't be built or run here. The patch's code is the same C++ for every platform (the Vulkan files, the shared command processor) and compiles with the Windows toolchains; nothing in it touches the static FidelityFX path. Not checked: a build there and anything running on either.

| Change | Files | Affects |
|---|---|---|
| The gate, the squeeze, the stretch decision, the LOD bias and ps_param_gen's scale, the sizes, the settings and the debug log | `include/rex/graphics/command_processor.h`, `src/graphics/temporal_aa.cpp` | all platforms |
| Viewport and scissor squeeze, system constants, sampler bias | `src/graphics/d3d12/command_processor.cpp`, `src/graphics/vulkan/command_processor.cpp`, `src/graphics/{d3d12,vulkan}/texture_cache.cpp`, `include/rex/graphics/{d3d12,vulkan}/command_processor.h`, `include/rex/graphics/{d3d12,vulkan}/texture_cache.h` | D3D12; Vulkan |
| The stretching resolves | `src/graphics/{d3d12,vulkan}/render_target_cache.cpp`, `include/rex/graphics/{d3d12,vulkan}/render_target_cache.h`, `include/rex/graphics/pipeline/render_target/cache.h`, `src/graphics/shaders/resolve_direct.xesli` | D3D12; Vulkan |
| The gather, FSR at the render size, frame generation's depth | `src/graphics/d3d12/temporal_aa.cpp`, `src/graphics/vulkan/temporal_aa.cpp`, `src/graphics/d3d12/frame_generation.cpp`, `src/graphics/vulkan/frame_generation.cpp` | D3D12; Vulkan |
| ps_param_gen's scale in the translators | `src/graphics/pipeline/shader/dxbc_translator.cpp`, `spirv_translator.cpp` and their headers | D3D12; Vulkan |
| The shaders | `src/graphics/shaders/taa_upscale_gather_mcla.cs.xesl`, `resolve_direct_{fast,full}_{32,64}bpp_stretch[_scaled].cs.xesl`, their headers under `bytecode/d3d12_5_1` and `vulkan_spirv` | D3D12; Vulkan |
| The presenter's CAS and FSR 1 scaling filters compiled in (`REX_HAS_FIDELITYFX_SDK`) | `src/ui/CMakeLists.txt`, `src/graphics/CMakeLists.txt` | Windows, D3D12 and Vulkan presenters |

## rexglue-guest-atomics-lock-free.patch

**Why.** The console's Interlocked helpers (33 functions in this game, 45 regions; the library's `InterlockedIncrement`, `InterlockedCompareExchange` and the like) are written as `mfmsr` / `mtmsrd r13` (interrupts off) / `lwarx` / compare / `stwcx.` / `mtmsrd` (interrupts back). The code generator turns the two `mtmsrd` into taking and releasing the SDK's global critical region lock (a recursive mutex shared by every thread and the kernel, the GPU command thread's shared-memory and texture-cache work included), and the `mfmsr` into taking it for a moment to see whether this thread holds it, plus a sequentially consistent fence at each; the `stwcx.` is a compare-and-swap on the host. Profiling the game (`launcher/dev/profiling-2026-10-26.md`): 6 to 8% of the render thread's samples in the city, 11% in a race (0.46 to 0.82 ms per swap), with the lock's contended path (`RtlEnterCriticalSection`, `RtlLeaveCriticalSection`) under them, and a timing-only experiment that made the three macros do nothing took 3 to 8% (0.13 to 0.32 ms per swap) off the render thread.

**What is exact, and why.** The lock around those regions protects nothing the compare-and-swap does not: a static check of the generated code of this game (`launcher/dev/check_atomic_regions.py`) finds that between each `mtmsrd r13` and the `mtmsrd` that ends it (on every path out, the compare-failed one included) there are only loads, additions, subtractions, compares and branches, and the store-conditional, which is `__sync_bool_compare_and_swap` (a locked instruction, a full barrier on x86); no other store, no call, no `mfmsr`, no second `mtmsrd r13`. The lock made other threads in such regions wait for each other, so that the compare-and-swap seldom failed; without it the compare-and-swap fails and the guest's own loop (`bne` back to the `lwarx`) goes round again, as the console's reservation does. The fences stay, so the ordering around the regions is what it was. `mfmsr` reads "interrupts enabled" (0x8000), which it did whenever the thread was not itself inside a region, and with no region taking the lock no thread is. What stops being excluded is only the guest's compare-and-swap against SDK code that holds the global critical region, which protects the SDK's own tables and not the words the guest's Interlocked helpers work on. A game with a region that holds anything else (a store, a call) must keep the lock: the check above has to pass on it first.

**What it changes.** `resources/templates/codegen/pch_h.inja` (the macros `REX_CHECK_GLOBAL_LOCK`, `REX_ENTER_GLOBAL_LOCK`, `REX_LEAVE_GLOBAL_LOCK`; only that file of the generated code changes, and `rexglue codegen` writes it at every build), and `global_critical_region::guest_regions_lock_free()`, which reads the cvar once. Cvar `guest_atomics_lock_free` (default on; read when the first region runs, so a change takes effect at the next start; off takes the lock as before). Built and run on Windows only; the PS5's list names the patch so that the lists stay in step.

**Measured (2026-10-27, `launcher/dev/profiling-2026-10-26.md`).** D3D12, native, uncapped, 1 warm-up and 3 runs a side in turn, the option off as the reference (the same runs as the timer queue section's table); per swap, free roam / standing / driving.
- 16 logical processors: the render thread 3.73 / 3.85 / 3.97 ms to 3.46 / 3.60 / 3.49 (-7 / -6 / -12%); the process 23.67 / 23.51 / 26.11 ms to 23.19 / 23.36 / 25.34 (-2 / -1 / -3%); the frame median 6.882 / 6.817 / 6.877 ms to 6.847 / 6.797 / 6.827 (-0.5 / -0.3 / -0.7%); p99 8.00 / 8.02 / 8.06 to 7.85 / 7.95 / 7.97 ms (-2.0 / -0.9 / -1.1%).
- 8 logical processors: the render thread 3.80 / 3.89 / 3.98 to 3.58 / 3.66 / 3.87 ms (-6 / -6 / -3%); the process 24.09 / 24.26 / 26.13 to 24.08 / 23.98 / 26.84 (0 / -1 / +3%; in the driving segment the main thread's CPU was 4% and the submission thread's 7% higher); the frame median -0.1 / -0.4 / -0.2%; p99 -0.5 / -0.4 / +0.1%.
- The race (16 processors): the render thread 5.65 to 5.37 ms (-5%); the process 36.19 to 35.85 (-1%); the frame median 6.855 to 6.840 ms (-0.2%); p99 8.59 to 8.49 (-1.2%); the main thread 5.36 to 5.68 ms.
- Against the unmodified SDK (the shipped build, 16 processors): the render thread -9%, the process -2%, the frame -0.5 / -0.5 / -0.4%, p99 -0.7 / +0.2 / -0.6%.
- 5 runs a side, the shipped defaults at 6 or more logical processors (this on, `timer_queue_hr_wait` off) against the unmodified SDK: 16 processors, the render thread 3.85 / 3.87 / 3.92 ms to 3.53 / 3.53 / 3.79 (-8 / -9 / -3%), the process -3 / -2 / 0%, the frame median -0.4 / -0.2 / -0.1%, p99 -0.3 / -0.4 / -0.7%; 8 processors, the render thread -7 / -7 / +1%, the process 0 / -2 / +4%, the frame +0.5 / -0.5 / -0.1%, p99 -0.3 / -0.6 / +1.9%; the race (3 runs a side), the render thread 5.87 to 5.23 ms (-11%), the process -1%, the frame 6.895 to 6.854 ms (-0.6%), p99 8.52 to 8.51.

The option passes the keep rule everywhere (the frame at most 1% longer, p99 at most 3%). The render thread's CPU falls by 0.1 to 0.5 ms per swap and the frame time by a few tenths of a percent at most: in these routes the frame is not limited by that thread's CPU time, so the gain is in CPU time, not in frame rate. It stays on.

| Change | Files | Affects |
|---|---|---|
| The three lock macros skip the lock, with a cvar read once | `resources/templates/codegen/pch_h.inja` | the generated code of every title |
| `guest_regions_lock_free()` and the cvar | `include/rex/thread/mutex.h`, `src/core/mutex.cpp` | all platforms |

## rexglue-resolve-direct-batched-loads.patch

**What was slow.** The direct resolve shaders of `rexglue-direct-resolve.patch` loaded a thread's samples one at a time. A thread copies 4 or 8 pixels, and the copies that average run over 1, 2 or 4 samples, in loops the shaders keep rolled (so the conversions are there once) with a conditional on the format inside; every load had to come back before the next was issued. A sample of a multisampled render target goes through its FMASK and metadata, so a load is a long round trip, and a GPU with few compute units does not have enough threads in flight to hide that. On the integrated Radeon of an AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D (2 RDNA2 compute units, shared DDR5) the resolves took 8.5 ms of a 39.7 ms GPU frame at native resolution, at 10 to 16 GB/s where a texture load decode reaches 50 GB/s on the same GPU, and 50.8 ms of 178.7 ms at 2x (4 times the pixels cost 6 times as much). On the RTX 5080 the loads are not what the resolves wait for.

**What it does.** With `resolve_direct_batched_loads` (default on; restart to change) the direct resolve pipelines are built from the same sources with `XE_RESOLVE_DIRECT_BATCHED_LOADS` (`src/graphics/shaders/resolve_direct_batched_*.cs.xesl`: the fast 32bpp and 64bpp and the full 32bpp and 64bpp copies, the ones this game uses, 16 shaders for each API; the full 8bpp and 16bpp copies keep the earlier shaders):
- a thread issues the loads of the pixels it converts, 4 or 8, in one run of instructions with nothing between them (the float view for colour up to 7e3 and for depth, the integer view for the integer formats, both for depth and stencil), and converts afterwards;
- the copies that average take their samples two at a time (one or two iterations for 1, 2 or 4 samples), the second sample's loads issued with the first's;
- and where all the raw values of the second sample's pixels are bit for bit the first's (most pixels of a multisampled picture: both samples are the same), the second conversion and unpacking is not done and the first's result is used again. The conversion is a function of the raw bits and of nothing else, so this is exact; it is the only change to the arithmetic, which is otherwise the original code in the original order.

Everything after the loads is the original code, so the result is the same, bit for bit (checked against the EDRAM buffer path, below). `false` builds the pipelines of the earlier shaders. The new shaders are larger, as the conversions are there once for every pixel loaded (56 KB of DXBC for the full 32bpp copy against 19 KB; the patch is 5.1 MB, mostly the shaders' bytecode headers, which `launcher/dev/build_resolve_shaders.py` rebuilds from the sources and `--check` verifies).

Measured on Windows, the integrated Radeon above (2 compute units), with the launcher's settings (2x MSAA), `--hdr=off`, GPU timestamps (`gpu-timestamps.sdk.patch`, `resolve-split-timestamps.sdk.patch`), free roam and driving, median of ten (Vulkan: twelve) frames, runs next to each other in the order off, on, on, off (this GPU has slow hours in which everything takes 1.4 times as long: runs from different hours are not compared; `launcher/dev/gpu-weak-2026-10-27.md`).

*Direct3D 12, native resolution* (the final shaders, 65 s runs; the first off run was in a slow hour and is left out; frame is swap to swap):

| Per frame, ms | Off | On, run 1 | On, run 2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Frame, free roam | 39.24 | 36.34 (-7.4%) | 36.74 (-6.4%) |
| Frame, drive | 38.20 | 34.94 (-8.5%) | 35.43 (-7.2%) |
| Resolves, free roam | 8.38 | 5.59 | 5.73 |
| 1280x720 depth, 2x MSAA, two a frame | 0.810, 0.676 | 0.483, 0.343 | 0.478, 0.345 |
| The four 640x640 4x MSAA depth cascades | 0.749, 0.706, 0.682, 0.636 | 0.403, 0.353, 0.332, 0.281 | 0.398, 0.369, 0.343, 0.296 |
| 1280x720 colour, 7e3 to 8_8_8_8 (the shadow mask) | 1.294 | 1.034 | 1.041 |
| 1280x720 colour, 10:10:10:2 to 16_16_16_16F (the HDR scene) | 1.234 | 1.100 | 1.082 |

The first version (the loads of a thread batched, the samples of the averaging copies one after the other) gave 8.50 and 8.38 ms of resolves off against 5.76 and 5.77 on, a frame of 39.7 and 39.5 off against 36.9 and 36.8, the shadow mask at 1.22 and the HDR colour at 1.08 to 1.12 ms: the depth copies gained what they could from it, the two averaging colour copies are bound by the arithmetic of the 7e3 conversion and unpacking, for every sample of every pixel, which two compute units get through at about 0.3 trillion operations a second. Loading two samples together did not change that (one run in a slow hour, with the same ratios). Skipping the second conversion took the shadow mask from 1.22 to 1.03 ms and left the HDR colour where it was (its pixels differ more between samples), 0.1 to 0.2 ms of the frame in all, which is under what two runs can tell apart; it is kept because it is exact by construction and checked (below), and costs nothing where the samples differ.

*Vulkan, native resolution* (the same shaders as SPIR-V, off, on, on-with-`vulkan_texture_load_storage_copy`, off-with-it): resolves 7.27 to 5.46 ms in free roam (7.24 to 5.44 driving), frame 35.70 to 33.89 ms (-5.1%; driving 35.23 to 33.63, -4.5%).

*Direct3D 12, 2x resolution* (one run each, on then off, the scaled resolve buffer): resolves 37.41 to 26.41 ms in free roam (37.19 to 25.86 driving, -29 to -30%), GPU time 142.6 to 139.1 ms (137.0 to 132.4 driving), but the frame the same, 141.6 against 141.7 ms (139.0 against 142.5 driving): the draws, the texture loads and the post chain took 2 to 4 ms longer in the run with the patch, as if the stall had moved to the work that follows the resolves, or one run was in a slightly slower hour. One pair of runs is not enough to say which; no gain at 2x is claimed (no loss either: the frame is within 2.5% in both segments).

*RTX 5080*, the GPU timestamps, off then on, 1x and 3x, Direct3D 12 and Vulkan (the card is bound by the game thread at 1x, 6.8 ms): resolves 0.32 and 0.30 ms at 1x, 1.17 and 1.02 ms at 3x on Direct3D 12 (-13%), 0.26 and 0.27 ms (1x), 0.97 and 0.92 ms (3x) on Vulkan; frame 6.83 and 6.83 ms (1x), 8.52 and 8.43 ms (3x) on Direct3D 12, 6.79 and 6.92 ms (1x), 8.13 and 7.89 ms (3x) on Vulkan. No regression: the resolve column is equal or lower in every pair (the Vulkan GPU totals at 1x move by a millisecond either way between two runs of the same code).

*Memory placement* (the question whether the resolves wait for where the shared memory lives; Direct3D 12, 1x, batched on): the shared memory as one committed buffer instead of the reserved, tiled one (`d3d12_tiled_shared_memory=false`) 36.33 ms against 36.82 and 36.91 for the default, textures committed instead of placed in heaps (`d3d12_texture_heaps=false`) 37.95 ms: neither is worth anything (both under 5%), so nothing was implemented for it.
